3
Global analysis on Riemannian manifolds

of constant curvature

In this paper, when we refer to a
fundamental solution
, it is meant to be a fundamental solution of a partial differential operator on a Riemannian manifold
M
. A fundamental solution for a linear partial differential operator
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\bf x}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\bf x}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bf x}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bf x}$}}}
on a
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold
M
, is in general a distribution
{\mathcal{G}}^{d}\colon(M\times M)\setminus\{({\bf x},{\bf x})\colon{\bf x}\in M\}\to{\mathbf{C}}
which satisfies the following linear partial differential equation

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\bf x}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\bf x}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bf x}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bf x}$}}}{\mathcal{G}}^{d}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle g$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle g$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle g$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle g$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}),

where
{\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\in M
,
g
is the Riemannian structure on
M
, and
\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle g$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle g$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle g$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle g$}}}
is the Dirac delta distribution on
M
. Note that for the operators that are treated in this paper, a fundamental solution will always be function, but for other operators such as for wave operators (see, e.g.,
[
18
, equation (4.1.5)]
), fundamental solutions are in general distributions. Furthermore, we will only perform global and local analysis for Riemannian manifolds
M
with constant curvature (hyperspherical
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, hyperbolic
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, and Euclidean geometry
{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
), and we will only study linear elliptic partial differential operators of the form
\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big)
, where
-\Delta
is the positive Laplace–Beltrami operator on
M
and
\beta^{2}\geq 0
,
R>0
. In this paper, these operators are referred to as Helmholtz operators, and in the case where
\beta=0
, they are Laplacian operators.

In this section we develop the necessary material in order to study fundamental solutions for these operators on these manifolds.

3.1
Hyperspherical geometry and the hyperboloid model

of hyperbolic geometry

Hyperbolic space in
d
-dimensions is a fundamental example of a space exhibiting hyperbolic geometry. It was developed independently by Lobachevsky and Bolyai around 1830 (see
[
34
]
), and most likely by Gauss and Schweikart (although they never published this result), even earlier (see
[
21
, Chapter 6]
). It is a geometry analogous to Euclidean geometry, but such that Euclid’s parallel postulate is no longer assumed to hold.

There are several models of
d
-dimensional hyperbolic space including the Klein, Poincaré, hyperboloid, upper-half space and hemisphere models (see
[
32
]
). The hyperboloid model for
d
-dimensional
hyperbolic geometry is closely related to the Klein and Poincaré models: each can be obtained projectively from the others. The upper-half space and hemisphere models can be obtained from one another by inversions with the Poincaré model (see
[
32
, Section 2.2]
). The model we will be focusing on in this paper is the hyperboloid model.

The hyperboloid model, also known as the Minkowski or Lorentz model, is a model of
d
-dimensional hyperbolic geometry in which points are represented by the upper sheet (submanifold)
S^{+}
of a two-sheeted hyperboloid
embedded in the Minkowski space
{\mathbf{R}}^{d,1}
. Minkowski space is a
(d+1)
-dimensional pseudo-Riemannian manifold which is a real finite-dimensional vector space, with coordinates given by
{\bf x}=(x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}},x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}},\ldots,x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}})
. It is equipped with a nondegenerate, symmetric bilinear form, the Minkowski bilinear form
[\cdot,\cdot]\colon{\mathbf{R}}^{d,1}\times{\mathbf{R}}^{d,1}\to{\mathbf{R}}
defined such that

\displaystyle[{\bf x},{\mathbf{y}}]:=x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}-x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}-\dots-x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}}y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}}.

The above bilinear form is symmetric, but not positive-definite, so it is not an inner product. It is defined analogously with the Euclidean inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)\colon{\mathbf{R}}^{d+1}\times{\mathbf{R}}^{d+1}\to{\mathbf{R}}
defined such that

\displaystyle({\bf x},{\mathbf{y}}):=x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}+x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}+\dots+x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}}y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}}.

The variety
[{\bf x},{\bf x}]=x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}^{2}-x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}^{2}-\dots-x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}}^{2}=R^{2}
, for
{\bf x}\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d,1}
, using the language of
[
2
]
(see also
[
35
, p. 504]
), defines a pseudo-sphere of radius
R
. Points on the pseudo-sphere with zero radius coincide with the cone. Points on the pseudo-sphere with radius greater than zero lie within this cone, and points on the pseudo-sphere with purely imaginary radius lie outside the cone. The upper sheets of the positive radii pseudo-spheres are maximally symmetric, simply connected, negative-constant sectional curvature (given by
-1/R^{2}
, see for instance
[
19
, p. 148]
),
d
-dimensional Riemannian submanifolds, embedded and with induced metric from the ambient Minkowski space
{\mathbf{R}}^{d,1}
. For
R\in(0,\infty)
, we refer to the upper sheet of this variety
[{\bf x},{\bf x}]=R^{2}
, with
{\bf x}\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d,1},
as the
R
-radius hyperboloid
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
.
Similarly, we refer to the variety
({\bf x},{\bf x})=R^{2}
for
R>0
and
{\bf x}\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d+1}
, as the
R
-radius hypersphere
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
which is a maximally symmetric, simply connected, positive-constant sectional curvature (given by
1/R^{2}
)
d
-dimensional Riemannian submanifold, embedded and with induced metric from the ambient Euclidean space. The Euclidean space
{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
equipped with the Pythagorean norm, is a space with vanishing curvature. We denote the unit radius hyperboloid by
{\mathbf{H}}^{d}:={\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}^{d}
and the unit radius hypersphere by
{\mathbf{S}}^{d}:={\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}^{d}
.

In our discussion of a fundamental solution for the Helmholtz operator in the hyperboloid model of hyperbolic geometry, we focus on the positive radius pseudo-sphere which can be parameterized through
subgroup-type coordinates,
i.e., those which correspond to a maximal subgroup chain
{\rm O}(d,1)\supset\cdots
(see for instance
[
28
]
). There exist separable coordinate systems which parameterize points on positive radius pseudo-spheres which can not be constructed using maximal subgroup chains, e.g., such as those which are analogous to parabolic coordinates, etc. We will no longer discuss these.

Geodesic polar coordinates are coordinates which correspond to the maximal subgroup chain given by
{\rm O}(d,1)\supset{\rm O}(d)\supset\cdots
. What we will refer to as
standard geodesic polar coordinates
correspond to the subgroup chain given by
{\rm O}(d,1)\supset{\rm O}(d)\supset{\rm O}(d-1)\supset\cdots\supset{\rm O}(2)
. Standard geodesic polar coordinates (see
[
14
,
25
]
), similar to standard hyperspherical coordinates in Euclidean space, can be given on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
by

\displaystyle x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}=R\cosh r,

\displaystyle x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}=R\sinh r\cos\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}},

\displaystyle x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}=R\sinh r\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}\cos\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-2$}}},

\displaystyle\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-2$}}}=R\sinh r\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}\cdots\cos\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}},

\displaystyle x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}=R\sinh r\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}\cdots\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}\cos\phi,

\displaystyle x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}}=R\sinh r\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}\cdots\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}\sin\phi,

(3.1)

where
r\in[0,\infty)
,
\phi\in[0,2\pi)
, and
\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle i$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle i$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle i$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}\in[0,\pi]
for
2\leq i\leq d-1
, and on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
by

\displaystyle x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}=R\cos\theta,

\displaystyle x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}=R\sin\theta\cos\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}},

\displaystyle x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}=R\sin\theta\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}\cos\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-2$}}},

\displaystyle\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-2$}}}=R\sin\theta\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}\cdots\cos\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}},

\displaystyle x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}=R\sin\theta\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}\cdots\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}\cos\phi,

\displaystyle x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}}=R\sin\theta\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}\cdots\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}\sin\phi,

(3.2)

where
\theta:=\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}}\in[0,\pi]
,
\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle i$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle i$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle i$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}\in[0,\pi]
for
2\leq i\leq d-1
, and
\phi\in[0,2\pi)
.

The isometry group of the space
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
is the pseudo-orthogonal group
{\rm SO}(d,1)
, the Lorentz group in
(d+1)
-dimensions. Hyperbolic space
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, can be identified with the quotient space
{\rm SO}(d,1)/{\rm SO}(d)
. The isometry group acts transitively on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
. That is, any point on the hyperboloid can be carried, with the help of a Euclidean rotation of
{\rm SO}(d-1)
, to the point
(\cosh\alpha,\sinh\alpha,0,\ldots,0)
,
and a hyperbolic rotation

\displaystyle x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}^{\prime}=-x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\sinh\alpha+x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}\cosh\alpha,\qquad x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}^{\prime}=-x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\cosh\alpha-x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}\sinh\alpha,

maps that point to the origin
(1,0,\ldots,0)
of the space.

In order to do analysis on a fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation on the hyperboloid and hypersphere, we need to describe how one computes distances in these spaces. One may naturally compare distances on the positive radius pseudo-sphere through analogy with the
R
-radius hypersphere. Distances on the hypersphere are simply given by arc lengths, angles between two arbitrary vectors, from the origin, in the ambient Euclidean space. We consider the
d
-dimensional hypersphere embedded in
{\mathbf{R}}^{d+1}
. Points on the hypersphere can be parameterized using hyperspherical coordinate systems. Any parameterization of the hypersphere
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, must have
({\bf x},{\bf x})=x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}^{2}+\dots+x_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}}^{2}=R^{2}
, with
R>0
. The geodesic distance between two points on the hypersphere
d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}\colon{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\times{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\to[0,\pi R]
is given by

\displaystyle d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}):=R\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}=R\cos^{-1}\left(\frac{({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})}{({\bf x},{\bf x})({{\bf x}^{\prime}},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})}\right)=R\cos^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{R^{2}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\right).

(3.3)

This is evident from the fact that the geodesics on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
are great circles (i.e., intersections of
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
with planes through the origin) with constant speed parameterizations (see
[
19
, p. 82]
).

Accordingly, we now look at the geodesic distance function on the
d
-dimensional positive radius pseudo-sphere
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
. Distances between two points on the positive radius pseudo-sphere are given by the hyperangle between two arbitrary vectors, from the origin, in the ambient Minkowski space. Any parameterization of the hyperboloid
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, must have
[{\bf x},{\bf x}]=R^{2}
. The geodesic distance
d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}\colon{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\times{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
between any two points on the hyperboloid is given by

\displaystyle d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}):=R\cosh^{-1}\left(\frac{[{\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}]}{[{\bf x},{\bf x}][{{\bf x}^{\prime}},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}]}\right)=R\cosh^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{R^{2}}[{\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}]\right),

(3.4)

where the inverse hyperbolic cosine with argument
x\in(1,\infty)
is given by
[
27
, equation (4.37.19)]
, (
2.23
). Geodesics on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
are great hyperbolas (i.e., intersections of
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
with planes through the origin) with constant speed parameterizations (see
[
19
, p. 84]
). We also define two global functions
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}\colon{\mathbf{H}}^{d}\times{\mathbf{H}}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
,
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}\colon{\mathbf{S}}^{d}\times{\mathbf{S}}^{d}\to[0,\pi]
which represent the projections of the global geodesic distance functions (
3.4
) on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
and
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
onto the corresponding unit radius hyperboloid
{\mathbf{H}}^{d}
and hypersphere
{\mathbf{S}}^{d}
respectively, namely

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}(\widehat{\bf x},{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}):=d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})/R,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}(\widehat{\bf x},{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}):=d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})/R,

(3.6)

where
\widehat{\bf x}={\bf x}/R
and
{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}={{\bf x}^{\prime}}/R
.

3.2
The Helmholtz equation in Riemannian spaces of constant curvature

Parametrizations of a submanifold embedded in either a Euclidean or Minkowski space are given in terms of coordinate systems whose coordinates are curvilinear. These are coordinates based on some transformation that converts the standard Cartesian coordinates in the ambient space to a coordinate system with the same number of coordinates as the dimension of the submanifold in which the coordinate lines are curved.

On a
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold
M
(a manifold together with a Riemannian metric
g
), the Laplace–Beltrami operator (Laplacian)
\Delta\colon C^{p}(M)\to C^{p-2}(M)
,
p\geq 2
, in curvilinear coordinates

{\mathbf{\xi}}=\big(\xi^{1},\ldots,\xi^{d}\big)
is given by

\displaystyle\Delta=\sum_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle i,j=1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle i,j=1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle i,j=1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle i,j=1$}}}^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|g|}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{i}}\left(\sqrt{|g|}g^{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{j}}\right),

(3.7)

where
|g|=|\det(g_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle ij$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle ij$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle ij$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle ij$}}})|,
the infinitesimal distance is given by

\displaystyle{\rm d}s^{2}=\sum_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle i,j=1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle i,j=1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle i,j=1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle i,j=1$}}}^{d}g_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle ij$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle ij$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle ij$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle ij$}}}{\rm d}\xi^{i}{\rm d}\xi^{j},

(3.8)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle i=1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle i=1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle i=1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle i=1$}}}^{d}g_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle ki$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle ki$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle ki$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle ki$}}}g^{ij}=\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle k$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}}^{j}.

For a Riemannian submanifold, the relation between the metric tensor in the ambient space and
g_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle ij$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle ij$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle ij$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle ij$}}}
of (
3.7
), (
3.8
) is

\displaystyle g_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle ij$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle ij$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle ij$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle ij$}}}({\mathbf{\xi}})=\sum_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle k,l=0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle k,l=0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle k,l=0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle k,l=0$}}}^{d}G_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle kl$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle kl$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle kl$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle kl$}}}\frac{\partial x^{k}}{\partial\xi^{i}}\frac{\partial x^{l}}{\partial\xi^{j}}.

On
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
the ambient space is Minkowski, and therefore
G=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,\ldots,-1)
. On
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
,
G=\operatorname{diag}(1,1,\ldots,1)
.

The set of all geodesic polar coordinate systems on the hyperboloid correspond to the many ways one can put coordinates on a hyperbolic hypersphere, i.e., the Riemannian submanifold
U\subset{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
defined for a bounded
{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
such that
d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=b={\rm const}
, where
b\in(0,\infty)
. These are coordinate systems which correspond to maximal subgroup chains starting with
{\rm O}(d,1)\supset{\rm O}(d)\supset\cdots
, with standard geodesic polar coordinates given by (
3.1
) being only one of them. (For a thorough description of these see
[
35
, Section X.5]
.) They all share the property that they are described by

d
-variables:
r\in[0,\infty)
plus
(d-1)
-angles each being given by the values
[0,2\pi)
,
[0,\pi]
,
[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
or
[0,\pi/2]
(see
[
15
,
16
]
).

In any of the geodesic polar coordinate systems, the global geodesic distance between any two points on the hyperboloid and hypersphere are given by (cf. (
3.4
), (
3.6
))

\displaystyle d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=R\cosh^{-1}\bigl(\cosh r\cosh r^{\prime}-\sinh r\sinh r^{\prime}\cos\gamma\bigr),

(3.9)

\displaystyle d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=R\cos^{-1}\bigl(\cos\theta\cos\theta^{\prime}+\sin\theta\sinh\theta^{\prime}\cos\gamma\bigr),

(3.10)

where
\gamma
is the unique separation angle defined on the unit radius submanifold
{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}
which is embedded in both
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifolds
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
and
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
for a fixed geodesic radius. For instance, one may write the separation angle in standard geodesic polar coordinates (
3.1
) or (
3.2
) as follows

\displaystyle\cos\gamma=\cos(\phi-\phi^{\prime})\prod_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle i=1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle i=1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle i=1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle i=1$}}}^{d-2}\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle i$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle i$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle i$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}{\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle i$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle i$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle i$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}}^{\prime}+\sum_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle i=2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle i=2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle i=2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle i=2$}}}^{d-2}\cos\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle i$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle i$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle i$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}{\cos\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle i$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle i$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle i$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}}^{\prime}\prod_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle j=i+1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle j=i+1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle j=i+1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle j=i+1$}}}^{d-2}\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle j$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle j$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle j$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}}}{\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle j$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle j$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle j$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}}}}^{\prime}.

(3.11)

Corresponding separation angle formulae for any geodesic polar coordinate system can be computed using (
3.3
), (
3.4
), and the associated formulae for the appropriate inner-products.

Note that by making use of the isometry group
{\rm SO}(d,1)
to map
{{\bf x}^{\prime}}
to the origin, then the geodesic distance
\rho
as measured from the origin to a point
{\bf x}\in{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
with curvilinear coordinate
r
is
\rho=Rr
. Hence if
R=1
which corresponds to
{\mathbf{H}}^{d}
(the unit radius hyperboloid), then on this Riemannian manifold, the geodesic distance is
\rho=r
, and there is no distinction between the global geodesic distance and the
r
-parameter in a geodesic polar coordinate system.

The infinitesimal distance in a geodesic polar coordinate system on the submanifold
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
is given by

\displaystyle{\rm d}s^{2}=R^{2}\big({\rm d}r^{2}+\sinh^{2}r{\rm d}\gamma^{2}\big),

(3.12)

and on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
by

\displaystyle{\rm d}s^{2}=R^{2}\big({\rm d}\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta{\rm d}\gamma^{2}\big),

(3.13)

where an appropriate expression for
\gamma
in a curvilinear coordinate system is given by (
3.11
). If one combines (
3.1
) or (
3.2
), (
3.7
), (
3.11
)
and (
3.12
) or (
3.13
), then in a particular geodesic polar coordinate system, the Helmholtz equation on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
is given by

\displaystyle\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big)f=\frac{1}{R^{2}}\left[-\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial r^{2}}-(d-1)\coth r\frac{\partial f}{\partial r}-\frac{1}{\sinh^{2}r}\Delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}}f\pm\beta^{2}R^{2}f\right]=0,

(3.14)

and on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
by

\displaystyle\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big)f=\frac{1}{R^{2}}\left[-\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial\theta^{2}}-(d-1)\cot\theta\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}-\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\Delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}}f\pm\beta^{2}R^{2}f\right]=0,

(3.15)

where
\Delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}}
is the corresponding Laplace–Beltrami operator on the unit radius hypersphere
{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}
.

3.3
Homogeneous solutions of the Helmholtz equation

in geodesic polar coordinates

Geodesic polar coordinate systems partition
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
into a family of
(d-1)
-dimensional hyperbolic hyperspheres, each with a geodesic radius
Rr
with
r\in(0,\infty)
on which all possible hyperspherical coordinate systems for
{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}
may be used (see for instance
[
35
]
). One then must also consider the limiting case for
r=0
to fill out all of
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
. In subgroup-type coordinate systems, one can compute the normalized hyperspherical harmonics in that space by solving the Laplace equation using separation of variables. This results in a general procedure which is given explicitly in
[
15
,
16
]
. These angular harmonics are given as general expressions involving trigonometric functions, Gegenbauer polynomials and Jacobi polynomials.

The harmonics in geodesic polar coordinate systems are given in terms of a radial solution multiplied by the angular harmonics. The angular harmonics are eigenfunctions of the Laplace–Beltrami operator on
{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}
which satisfy the following eigenvalue problem

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}}Y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle l$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}^{K}(\widehat{\bf x})=-l(l+d-2)Y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle l$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}^{K}(\widehat{\bf x}),

where
\widehat{\bf x}\in{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}
,
Y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle l$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}^{K}(\widehat{\bf x})
are normalized hyperspherical harmonics,
l\in{\mathbf{N}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}
is the angular momentum quantum number, and
K
stands for the set of
(d-2)
-quantum numbers identifying degenerate harmonics for each
l
. The degeneracy
(2l+d-2)(d-3+l)!/(l!(d-2)!)
(see
[
35
, equation (9.2.11)]
), tells you how many linearly independent solutions exist for a particular
l
value and dimension
d
. The hyperspherical harmonics can optionally be normalized so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}}Y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle l$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}^{K}(\widehat{\bf x})\overline{Y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle l^{\prime}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle l^{\prime}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle l^{\prime}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l^{\prime}$}}}^{K^{\prime}}(\widehat{\bf x})}\mathrm{d}\omega=\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle l$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}^{l^{\prime}}\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle K$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle K$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle K$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle K$}}}^{K^{\prime}},

where
d\omega
is the Riemannian (volume) measure (see for instance
[
13
, Section 3.4]
) on
{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}
which is invariant under the isometry group
{\rm SO}(d)
(cf. (
4.7
)), and for
x+{\rm i}y=z\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
\overline{z}=x-{\rm i}y
, represents complex conjugation. The generalized Kronecker delta
\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle K$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle K$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle K$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle K$}}}^{K^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}
is defined such that it equals 1 if all of the
(d-2)
-quantum numbers identifying degenerate harmonics for each
l
coincide, and equals zero otherwise.

Since the angular solutions (hyperspherical harmonics) are well-known (see for instance
[
35
, Chapter IX]
,
[
11
, Chapter 11]
), we will now focus on the radial solutions on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
and
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}

coming from
\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big)
in geodesic polar coordinate systems. These radial solutions respectively satisfy the following ordinary differential equations (cf. (
3.14
)) for all
R\in(0,\infty)
, namely,

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm d}^{2}h}{{\rm d}r^{2}}-(d-1)\coth r\frac{{\rm d}h}{{\rm d}r}+\left(\frac{l(l+d-2)}{\sinh^{2}r}+\beta^{2}R^{2}\right)h=0,

(3.16)

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm d}^{2}h}{{\rm d}r^{2}}-(d-1)\coth r\frac{{\rm d}h}{{\rm d}r}+\left(\frac{l(l+d-2)}{\sinh^{2}r}-\beta^{2}R^{2}\right)h=0,

(3.17)

on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
and

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm d}^{2}s}{{\rm d}\theta^{2}}-(d-1)\cot\theta\frac{{\rm d}s}{{\rm d}\theta}+\left(\frac{l(l+d-2)}{\sin^{2}\theta}+\beta^{2}R^{2}\right)s=0,

(3.18)

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm d}^{2}s}{{\rm d}\theta^{2}}-(d-1)\cot\theta\frac{{\rm d}s}{{\rm d}\theta}+\left(\frac{l(l+d-2)}{\sin^{2}\theta}-\beta^{2}R^{2}\right)s=0,

(3.19)

on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
.

Eight solutions to the ordinary differential equation (
3.16
)
h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm},h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}\colon(0,\infty)\to{\mathbf{C}}
are given by

\displaystyle h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}(r)=\frac{1}{\sinh^{\frac{d}{2}-1}r}P_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cosh r),

\displaystyle h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}(r)=\frac{1}{\sinh^{\frac{d}{2}-1}r}Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cosh r).

Similarly, if
(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}\geq 0
, the solutions to (
3.17
)
f_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1,\beta,R,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1,\beta,R,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1,\beta,R,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,\beta,R,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm},f_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}\colon(0,\infty)\to{\mathbf{C}}
are

\displaystyle f_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}(r)=\frac{1}{\sinh^{\frac{d}{2}-1}r}P_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cosh r),

\displaystyle f_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}(r)=\frac{1}{\sinh^{\frac{d}{2}-1}r}Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cosh r).

If
(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}\leq 0
, the solutions to (
3.17
) are given in terms of the associated Legendre conical functions
[
27
, Section 14.20]

\displaystyle y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}(r)=\frac{1}{\sinh^{\frac{d}{2}-1}r}P_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cosh r),

\displaystyle y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}(r)=\frac{1}{\sinh^{\frac{d}{2}-1}r}Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cosh r).

On
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, solutions to (

)

are given by

\displaystyle s_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}(\theta)=\frac{1}{\sin^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\theta}\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\pm\cos\theta),

\displaystyle s_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}(\theta)=\frac{1}{\sin^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\theta}\mathsf{Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\pm\cos\theta),

where
\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{\mu},\mathsf{Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{\mu}\colon(-1,1)\to{\mathbf{C}}
are Ferrers functions of the first and second kind
[
27
, Section 14.3(i)]
. Similarly, if

, solutions to (

)

w_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm},w_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}\colon(0,\pi)\to{\mathbf{C}}
are

\displaystyle w_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}(\theta)=\frac{1}{\sin^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\theta}\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\pm\cos\theta),

\displaystyle w_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}(\theta)=\frac{1}{\sin^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\theta}\mathsf{Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\pm\cos\theta).

Otherwise, if
(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}\leq 0
, the solutions to (

) are given in terms of Ferrers conical functions
[
27
, Section 14.20]

\displaystyle u_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}(\theta)=\frac{1}{\sin^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\theta}\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\pm\cos\theta),

\displaystyle u_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2,R,\beta,\pm$}}}^{d,l,\pm}(\theta)=\frac{1}{\sin^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\theta}\mathsf{Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\pm\cos\theta).

Remark 3.1
.

If
\frac{d}{2}-1+l
is a half odd integer (
d
odd), the solutions can be expressed as elementary functions. From
[
27
, equations (14.5.15)–(14.5.17)]
, then
P_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cosh r)
and
Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cosh r)
with odd
d
can be found by using the order-recurrence relation
[
27
, equation (14.10.6)]
. From
[
27
, equations (14.5.11)–(14.5.14)]
, then
{\sf P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cos\theta)
and
{\sf Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cos\theta)
with odd
d
can be found by using the order-recurrence relation
[
27
, equation (14.10.1)]
. On the other hand, if
d
is even and
(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}=0
, the solutions to (
3.16
), (
3.18
) can be written in terms of complete elliptic integrals of the first and second kind
K\colon[0,1)\to[\tfrac{\pi}{2},\infty)
and
E\colon[0,1]\to[1,\tfrac{\pi}{2}]
respectively. These are given in terms of the Gauss hypergeometric function, namely
[
27
, equations (19.5.1) and (19.5.2)]
or through definite integrals
[
27
, Section 19.2]
.
Using
[
27
, equations (14.5.24)–(14.5.27)]
, then,
P_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}^{1}(\cosh r)
is obtained from
P_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}(\cosh r)
with
P_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}(\cosh r)
, and
Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}^{1}(\cosh r)
from
Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}(\cosh r)
with
Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}(\cosh r)
, by the recurrence relations
[
27
, equation (14.10.7)]
. Using
[
27
, equations (14.5.20)–(14.5.23)]
, then,
\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}^{1}(\cos\theta)
is obtained from
\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}(\cos\theta)
with
\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}(\cos\theta)
, and
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}^{1}(\cos\theta)
from
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}(\cos\theta)
with
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}(\cos\theta)
, by the recurrence relations
[
27
, equation (14.10.2)]
. Subsequently, the recurrence relation
[
27
, equation (14.10.6)]
can be used to find
P_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cosh r)
and
Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cosh r)
in terms of complete elliptic integrals for all half odd integer values of
\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)
by starting with
P_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}(\cosh r)
,
P_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}^{1}(\cosh r)
,
Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}(\cosh r)
, and
Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}^{1}(\cosh r)
. Similarly,
[
27
, equation (14.10.1)]
can be used to find
\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cos\theta)
and
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}^{\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)}(\cos\theta)
in terms of complete elliptic integrals for all half odd integer values
of
\pm(\frac{d}{2}-1+l)
by starting with
\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}(\cos\theta)
,
\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}^{1}(\cos\theta)
,
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}(\cos\theta)
, and
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}$}}}^{1}(\cos\theta)
.

4
A fundamental solution of
\boldsymbol{\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big)}
in Riemannian spaces

of constant curvature

Due to the fact that the spaces
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
and
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
are homogeneous with respect to their isometry groups, the pseudo-orthogonal group
{\rm SO}(d,1)
of
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
and the orthogonal group
{\rm O}(d)
of
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, and therefore isotropic manifolds, we expect that there exists a fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation on each space with spherically symmetric dependence. We specifically expect these solutions to be given on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
in terms of the associated Legendre function of the second kind with argument given by
\cosh r
. This associated Legendre function naturally fits our requirements because it is singular at
r=0
and vanishes at infinity, whereas the associated Legendre function of the first kind, with the same argument, is regular at
r=0
and singular at infinity. One also might expect a fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation on hyperspheres to be a Ferrers function of the second kind with argument given by
\cos\theta
, since this is the result we have found previously for Laplace’s equation (see (
4.11
) below). However on hyperspheres for the Helmholtz equation, there is a twist, as we will see in the next subsection.

4.1
Properties of fundamental solutions

of inhomogeneous Laplace/Helmholtz equations on
\boldsymbol{{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}}

In computing a fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation, we know that on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
,
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
respectively, a fundamental solution of Helmholtz operators satisfy

\displaystyle\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big){\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}),

(4.1)

\displaystyle\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big){\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}),

(4.2)

where
h
and
s
are Riemannian metrics on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
and
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
respectively, and
\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})
and
\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})
are the corresponding Dirac delta distributions on those Riemannian manifolds.

Remark 4.1
.

Note that solutions to the inhomogeneous Helmholtz equation corresponding to the operator
\big({-}\Delta-\beta^{2}\big)
on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
exist except at a countably infinite number of ‘bad’ positive values of
\beta^{2}
. These correspond to the eigenvalues of the Laplace–Beltrami operator on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
and are given by
\nu\in{\mathbf{N}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}
. Also note that Szmytkowski
[
31
]
has studied a
modified
or
reduced
fundamental solution to deal with this case, but we have not yet pursued this.

Consider Poisson’s equation on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
,
-\Delta u=\rho
on a compact Riemannian manifold
M
with boundary
\partial M
. The divergence theorem on this manifold is given by (cf.
[
19
, p. 43]
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle M$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}}\operatorname{div}{\bf X}{\rm d}V=\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\partial M$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\partial M$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\partial M$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\partial M$}}}\langle{\bf X},{\bf N}\rangle\mathrm{d}{\tilde{V}},

(4.3)

where
{\rm d}V
is the Riemannian volume measure on
M
,
{\bf N}
is the outward unit normal to
\partial M
, and
{\rm d}{\tilde{V}}
is the Riemannian volume measure of the induced metric on
\partial M
. If one invokes the divergence theorem on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, with regard to Poisson’s equation on this manifold using
{\bf X}=\nabla u
, then since
\partial{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}=\varnothing
, one ascertains

\displaystyle\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}}\rho\mathrm{d}V=0.

Hence on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
(and on all compact manifolds without boundary), there does not exist a source density distribution
\rho
, satisfying Poisson’s equation, with non-vanishing integral. In fact, a fundamental solution of Laplace’s equation on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
(
4.11
), as pointed out in
[
3
, Section 5.4]
is actually the solution to the Poisson equation whose inhomogeneous source distribution is given by a point source at the
origin and another with opposite sign, on the opposite pole of the hypersphere (both modeled by Dirac delta distributions).

Applying the divergence theorem to solutions of the inhomogeneous Helmholtz equations
\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big)u=\rho
on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
produces

\displaystyle\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}}\rho\mathrm{d}V=\pm\beta^{2}\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}}u\mathrm{d}V.

(4.4)

Hence for the Helmholtz equation, the integral of the density distribution over the entire manifold is not required to vanish. Instead, it must be equal to the total integral of the solution multiplied by the wavenumber
\pm\beta^{2}
. Note that in the case where the total integral of the density distribution vanishes, this implies that the total integral of the solution must also vanish. This is the case for a fundamental solution of Laplace’s equation on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, in which case a fundamental solution must be an odd function of the geodesic distance as measured from the equator
\theta=\tfrac{1}{2}\pi
of the hypersphere
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
. We will also study odd solutions for Helmholtz operators on these manifolds and compare the
\beta\to 0^{+}
limit for Laplace operators for the opposite antipodal fundamental solutions

\displaystyle\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big){\mathcal{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})-\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}(-{\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}),

(4.5)

whose total integral over the manifold must vanish.

Another important requirement for a fundamental solution of the
\big({-}\Delta-\beta^{2}\big)
Helmholtz operator in Euclidean space is the
Sommerfeld radiation condition
. Sommerfeld defined this radiation condition for a scalar field satisfying the Helmholtz equation as
[
30
, p. 189]
(see also
[
29
, p. 396]
) “… the sources must be
sources
, not
sinks
of energy. The energy which is radiated from the sources must scatter to infinity;
no energy may be radiated from infinity into the prescribed singularities of the field
.” On
{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
, this radiation condition is expressed mathematically as
