\displaystyle\lim_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle r\to\infty$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle r\to\infty$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle r\to\infty$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle r\to\infty$}}}r^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial r}-{\rm i}\beta\right){\mathcal{G}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta$}}}^{d,-}(r)=0,

(4.6)

where
r=\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|
,
{\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
. The fundamental solution
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta$}}}^{d,-}
satisfying this condition will be given in Theorem
4.2
below.

4.2
A fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation

in spaces of constant curvature

The Dirac delta distribution with metric
g
is defined for an open set
U\subset{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
with
{\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, or
U\subset{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
with
{\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle U$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle U$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle U$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle U$}}}\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle g$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle g$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle g$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle g$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle g$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle g$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle g$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle g$}}}=\begin{cases}1,&\mathrm{if}\ {{\bf x}^{\prime}}\in U,\\
0,&\mathrm{if}\ {{\bf x}^{\prime}}\notin U,\end{cases}

where
{\rm d}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle g$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle g$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle g$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle g$}}}
is the Riemannian (volume) measure, invariant under the isometry group
{\rm SO}(d,1)
of the Riemannian manifold
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
or
{\rm O}(d)
of
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, given in standard geodesic polar coordinates on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
by

\displaystyle{\rm d}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}=R^{d}\sinh^{d-1}r{\rm d}r{\rm d}\omega:=R^{d}\sinh^{d-1}r{\rm d}r\sin^{d-2}\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}\cdots\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}{\rm d}\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\cdots{\rm d}\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}},

(4.7)

and on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
by

\displaystyle{\rm d}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}=R^{d}\sin^{d-1}\theta{\rm d}\theta{\rm d}\omega:=R^{d}\sin^{d-1}\theta{\rm d}\theta\sin^{d-2}\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}\cdots\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}{\rm d}{\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}}\cdots{\rm d}\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}},

where
{\rm d}\omega
is the Riemannian volume measure on
{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}
. Notice that as
r\to 0^{+}
and
\theta\to 0^{+}
,
{\rm d}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}
and
{\rm d}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}
go to the Euclidean volume measure, invariant under the Euclidean motion group
{\rm E}(d)
, in standard geodesic polar coordinates. Therefore in standard geodesic polar coordinates, we have the following:

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\frac{\delta(r-r^{\prime})}{R^{d}\sinh^{d-1}r^{\prime}}\frac{\delta(\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}-\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}^{\prime})\delta(\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}-\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}^{\prime})\cdots\delta(\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}-\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}^{\prime})}{\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}^{\prime}\sin^{2}\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 3$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}}^{\prime}\cdots\sin^{d-2}\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\frac{\delta(\theta-\theta^{\prime})}{R^{d}\sin^{d-1}\theta^{\prime}}\frac{\delta(\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}-\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}^{\prime})\delta(\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}-\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}^{\prime})\cdots\delta(\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}-\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}^{\prime})}{\sin\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}^{\prime}\sin^{2}\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 3$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 3$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 3$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}}^{\prime}\cdots\sin^{d-2}\theta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d-1$}}}^{\prime}}.

In general since we can add any function that satisfies the homogeneous Helmholtz equation to a fundamental solution of the same equation and still have a fundamental solution, we will use this freedom to make our fundamental solution as simple as possible. It is reasonable to expect that there exists a particular spherically symmetric fundamental solution on each manifold (
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})
on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})
on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
) with pure radial (
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}(\widehat{\bf x},{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}})
(
3.5
) on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
or
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}(\widehat{\bf x},{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}})
(
3.6
) on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
) and constant angular dependence (invariant under rotations centered about the origin), due to the influence of the point-like nature of the Dirac delta distribution. For a spherically symmetric solution of the Helmholtz equation, the corresponding
\Delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}^{d-1}$}}}
term vanishes since only the
l=0
term survives. In other words, we expect there to exist a fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation such that, aside from a multiplicative constant which depends on
R
,
\beta
, and
d
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=f_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}(\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}})
on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=f_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}(\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})
on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
.

In the remainder of this section, we derive fundamental solutions for
\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big)
on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
(Section
4.3
),
\big({-}\Delta+\beta^{2}\big)
on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
(Section
4.4.1
), and study two candidate fundamental solutions for
\big({-}\Delta-\beta^{2}\big)
on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
(Section
4.4.2
). We will require a fundamental solution of Helmholtz operators in Euclidean space
{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
. These are well-known and can be found in, for instance,
[
4
, p. 139]
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
d\in{\mathbf{N}}
. Define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\left(\frac{\beta}{\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}K_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}(\beta\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|),

(4.8)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\frac{{\rm i}}{4}\left(\frac{\beta}{2\pi\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}^{(1)}(\beta\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|),

(4.9)

then
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}
respectively are fundamental solutions for
\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big)
in Euclidean space
{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
, where
\Delta
is the Laplace operator on
{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
.

Note most authors only present the above theorem for the case
d\geq 2
but it is easily verified to also be valid for the case
d=1
as well.
Fundamental solutions for
-\Delta
on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
,
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
(opposite antipodal) are respectively (see
[
6
, Theorem 3.1]
,
[
7
, equation (2.4)]
)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}):=\frac{{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}\pi(\frac{d}{2}-1)}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}\sinh^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}}Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\cosh\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}),

(4.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}):=\frac{(d-2)!}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}\sin^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}}{\sf Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}^{1-\frac{d}{2}}(\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}),

(4.11)

where the geodesic distances are given by (
3.9
), (
3.10
). These fundamental solutions will be useful to constrain the possible homogeneous solutions which are to be used to obtain our fundamental solutions in the
\beta\to 0^{+}
limit (when they exist, see Section
4.1
).

Due to the fact that the spaces
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
and
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
are homogeneous with respect to their isometry groups
{\rm SO}(d,1)
and
{\rm O}(d)
respectively, and therefore isotropic manifolds, without loss of generality, we are free to map the point
{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
or
{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
to the origin. In this case, the global geodesic distance function
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}
coincides with the radial parameter
r
in geodesic polar coordinates, and
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}
with
\theta
; and therefore we may interchange
r
with
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}
and
\theta
with
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}
accordingly (cf. (
3.9
) with
r^{\prime}=0
) in our representation of a fundamental solution for the Helmholtz equation on each manifold. Notice that we can add any homogeneous solution to a fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equations (
3.14
), (
3.15
), respectively, and still have a fundamental solution of Helmholtz operators. This is because a fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation must satisfy

\displaystyle\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}}\left(-\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\right)(\varphi_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({{\bf x}^{\prime}})){\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}^{\prime}=\varphi_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({\bf x}),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}}\left(-\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\right)(\varphi_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}({{\bf x}^{\prime}}))\mathcal{A}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}^{\prime}=\varphi_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}({\bf x}),

for all
\varphi_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}\in{\mathcal{D}}({\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d})
,
\varphi_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}\in{\mathcal{D}}({\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d})
, where
{\mathcal{D}}
is the space of test functions, and
{\rm d}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h,s$}}}^{\prime}
are the Riemannian (volume) measures on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
,
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
respectively in the primed coordinates.

4.3
Fundamental solutions of Helmholtz operators on
\boldsymbol{{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}}

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
d\in\{2,3,\ldots\}
. Define
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}\colon({\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\times{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d})\setminus\{({\bf x},{\bf x})\colon{\bf x}\in{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\}\to{\mathbf{R}}
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}):=\frac{{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}\pi(\frac{d}{2}-1)}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}(\sinh\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\cosh\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}),

where
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}:=\cosh^{-1}([\widehat{\bf x},{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}])
is the geodesic distance between
\widehat{\bf x}
and
{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}
on the pseudo-sphere of unit radius
{\mathbf{H}}^{d}
, with
\widehat{\bf x}={\bf x}/R
,
{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}={{\bf x}^{\prime}}/R
. Then
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}
is a fundamental solution for the Helmholtz operator
\big({-}\Delta+\beta^{2}\big)
on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
.

Remark 4.4
.

It has been brought to our attention by one of the referees that a fundamental solution for the Helmholtz equation
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})
on the unit-radius hyperboloid
{\mathbf{H}}^{d}
is given in
[
24
, Theorem 3.3]
. Note that the half-space model of hyperbolic geometry is used there, rather than the hyperboloid model which is used in this paper.

Proof.

Our derivation for a fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})
on the
R
-radius hyperboloid
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
is as follows. By starting with the spherically symmetric solution

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sinh^{\frac{d}{2}-1}r}Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\cosh r),

which is singular at
r=0
, to the homogeneous the Helmholtz equation, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\frac{c}{\sinh^{\frac{d}{2}-1}r}Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\cosh\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}).

The constant
c
is obtained by matching to a Euclidean fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation in the flat-space limit
R\to\infty
(see
[
7
, Section 2.4]
) with geodesic distance
R\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}
and thus
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}\to 0^{+}
. Using (
2.13
),
\nu=-{\tfrac{1}{2}}+{\tfrac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}\sim\beta R\to\infty
, as
R\to\infty
and
\mu=\frac{d}{2}-1
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\sim\frac{c}{\sinh^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\rho)}{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\pi\mu}\nu^{\mu}\left(\frac{\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}}{\sinh\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}}\right)^{{\frac{1}{2}}}K_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}}}\left((\nu+{\tfrac{1}{2}})\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}\right)

\displaystyle\hphantom{{\mathcal{H}}_{R,\beta}^{d,+}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^\prime})}{}\sim c{\rm e}^{{\rm i}(\frac{d}{2}-1)\pi}\beta^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\frac{R^{d-2}}{(R\rho)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}K_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}(\beta R\rho)

\displaystyle\hphantom{{\mathcal{H}}_{R,\beta}^{d,+}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^\prime})}{}\sim c{\rm e}^{{\rm i}(\frac{d}{2}-1)\pi}\beta^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\frac{R^{d-2}}{\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}K_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}(\beta\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|),

(4.12)

where we have used
\sinh\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}\sim\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}
as
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}\to 0
. By equating (
4.12
) with
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta$}}}^{d,+}
and solving for
c
, we obtain
c={\rm e}^{-{\rm i}\pi(\frac{d}{2}-1)}(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}R^{2-d}
, which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

For
d=2
, the particular choice of
\beta=\sqrt{3}/(2R)
, a fundamental solution for this Helmholtz operator
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}
reduces to an expression involving complete elliptic integrals of the first and second kind through
[
27
, equations (14.5.24)–(14.5.27)]
. This is also true for even
d\geq 2
with different choices of
\beta
, namely

\displaystyle\beta=\frac{\sqrt{(n+1)^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}}{2R},

where
n
is an odd-integer, so that the associated Legendre function of the second kind becomes a toroidal harmonic. All toroidal harmonics can be obtained, for instance, by using
[
27
, equations (14.5.24)–(14.5.27)]
as starting points for the recurrence relations for associated Legendre functions of the second kind such as
[
27
, equations (14.10.3), (14.10.6) and (14.10.7)]
.

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
d\in\{2,3,\ldots\}.
Define
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}\colon({\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\times{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d})\setminus\{({\bf x},{\bf x})\colon{\bf x}\in{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\}\to{\mathbf{R}}
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}):=\frac{{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}\pi(\frac{d}{2}-1)}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}(\sinh\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\cosh\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}),

where
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}:=\cosh^{-1}\left([\widehat{\bf x},{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}]\right)
is the geodesic distance between
\widehat{\bf x}
and
{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}
on the pseudo-sphere of unit radius
{\mathbf{H}}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\nu=\begin{cases}-{\tfrac{1}{2}}+{\tfrac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}},&\mathrm{if}\ (d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}\geq 0,\\
-{\tfrac{1}{2}}-\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}},&\mathrm{if}\ (d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}\leq 0,\end{cases}

(4.13)

\widehat{\bf x}={\bf x}/R
,
{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}={{\bf x}^{\prime}}/R
. Then
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
is a fundamental solution for the Helmholtz operator
\big({-}\Delta-\beta^{2}\big)
on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
.

Proof.

Our derivation for a fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})
on the
R
-radius hyperboloid
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
is as follows. In the flat-space limit
R\to\infty
, we must start with a solution such that
4\beta^{2}R^{2}>(d-1)^{2}
. Therefore we look for spherically symmetric conical solutions of the form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sinh^{\frac{d}{2}-1}r}Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\cosh r),

(singular at
r=0
), of the homogeneous Helmholtz equation. Therefore we must decide which conical form

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\frac{c}{\sinh^{\frac{d}{2}-1}r}Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}\pm{\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}$}}}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\cosh\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}),

where
4\beta^{2}R^{2}>(d-1)^{2}
, is appropriate. Using Theorem
4.2
, we know the flat-space limit should produce a Hankel function of the first kind
H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}^{(1)}
, and therefore from Theorem
2.5
, we see that the solution with the minus sign in front of the imaginary unit is necessary. The constant
c
is obtained by matching to a Euclidean fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation in the flat-space limit
R\to\infty
with geodesic distance
R\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}
and thus
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}\to 0^{+}
. In the flat-space limit
\tau\sim\beta R
and
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}\sim r/R
, where
r
is the geodesic distance between two points in Euclidean space
{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
. Using (
2.22
) (with the plus sign in the order), as
R\to\infty
and
\mu=\frac{d}{2}-1
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\sim\frac{{\rm i}\pi c{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\pi\mu}\tau^{\mu}}{2(\sinh\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}\sqrt{\frac{\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}}{\sinh\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}}}H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}}}^{(1)}(\tau\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}})

\displaystyle\hphantom{{\mathcal{H}}_{R,\beta}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^\prime})}{}\sim\frac{c{\rm i}\pi{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\pi(\frac{d}{2}-1)}\beta^{\frac{d}{2}-1}R^{d-2}}{2r^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}H^{(1)}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}(\beta r)

\displaystyle\hphantom{{\mathcal{H}}_{R,\beta}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^\prime})}{}=\frac{c{\rm i}\pi{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\pi(\frac{d}{2}-1)}\beta^{\frac{d}{2}-1}R^{d-2}}{2\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}H^{(1)}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}(\beta\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|).

(4.14)

By equating (
4.14
) with
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta$}}}^{d,-}
in Theorem
4.2
and solving for
c
, we obtain

\displaystyle c=\frac{{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}(\frac{d}{2}-1)\pi}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}},

which proves the full behavior of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
for
4\beta^{2}R^{2}>(d-1)^{2}
. For the case
4\beta^{2}R^{2}\leq(d-1)^{2}
, we match up to a fundamental solution of
-\Delta
on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
(
4.10
) as
\beta\to 0
which requires a fundamental solution with functional dependence as follows

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\frac{g}{\sinh^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}}Q_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-{\frac{1}{2}}+{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}}$}}}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\cosh\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}).

Matching the constants
c
and
g
at
\beta=(d-1)/(2R)
, completes the proof.
∎

4.3.1
Uniqueness of fundamental solutions for
\boldsymbol{\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big)}
on
\boldsymbol{{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}}

It is clear that, in general, a fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation in the hyperboloid model of hyperbolic geometry
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}
is not unique since one can add any homogeneous solution of the Helmholtz equations
h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}\colon{\bf H}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\to{\mathbf{R}}
to
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}
and still obtain solutions to (
4.1
) since
h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}
is in the kernel of
\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big)
.

Proposition 4.7
.

There exists precisely one
C^{\infty}
-function
H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}\colon({\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\times{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d})\setminus\{({\bf x},{\bf x})\colon{\bf x}\in{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\}\to{\mathbf{R}}
such that for all
{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
the function

(H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}})_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}}\colon{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\setminus\{{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\}\to{\mathbf{R}}
defined by
(H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}})_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}}({\bf x}):=(H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}})({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})
is a function on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
with
\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big)(H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}})_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}}=\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle g$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle g$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle g$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle g$}}}(\cdot,{{\bf x}^{\prime}})
, and

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\to\infty$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\to\infty$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\to\infty$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\to\infty$}}}(H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}})_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}}({\bf x})=0,

(4.15)

where
d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})
is the geodesic distance between two points
{\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
.

Proof.

Existence is clear. For uniqueness, suppose
H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}
and
\tilde{H}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}
are two such functions. Let
{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
. Define the
C^{\infty}
-function
h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}\colon{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\setminus\{{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\}\to{\mathbf{R}}
by
h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}=(H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}})_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}}-({\tilde{H}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}})}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\bf x}^{\prime}}$}}}
. Then
h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}
is a function on
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
with
\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big)h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}=0
. Since
{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
is locally Euclidean, one has by local elliptic regularity that
h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}
can be extended to a
C^{\infty}
-function
\hat{h}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}\colon{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\to{\mathbf{R}}
. It follows from (
4.15
) for
H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}
and
\tilde{H}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}
that

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h,s$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\to\infty$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h,s$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\to\infty$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h,s$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\to\infty$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle d_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle h,s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle h,s$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\to\infty$}}}\hat{h}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}({\bf x})=0.

(4.16)

The strong elliptic maximum/minimum principle on a Riemannian manifold for a bounded domain
\Omega
states that if
u
is a homogeneous solution to an elliptic operator, then the supremum/infimum of
u
in
\Omega
coincides with the supremum/infimum of
u
on the boundary
\partial\Omega
. By using a compact exhaustion sequence
\Omega_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle k$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}}
in a non-compact connected Riemannian manifold and passing to a subsequence
{\bf x}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle k$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}}\in\partial\Omega_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle k$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}}
such that
{\bf x}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle k$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}}\to\infty
, the strong elliptic maximum/minimum principle can be extended to non-compact connected Riemannian manifolds with boundary conditions at infinity (see for instance
[
13
, Section 8.3.2]
). Taking
\Omega_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle k$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle k$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}}\subset{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, the strong elliptic maximum/minimum principle for non-compact connected Riemannian manifolds implies using (
4.16
) that
\hat{h}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}=0
. Therefore
h_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}=0
and
H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\tilde{H}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})
for all
{\bf x}\in{\mathbf{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\setminus\{{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\}
.
∎

By Proposition
4.7
, for
d\geq 2
, the functions
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}
are the unique fundamental solutions for these Helmholtz operators which satisfy the vanishing decay (
4.15
).

4.4
Fundamental solutions of Helmholtz operators
\boldsymbol{\big({-}\Delta\pm\beta^{2}\big)}
on
\boldsymbol{{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}}

It has been discussed in Section
4.1
that on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
there exists fundamental solutions of Helmholtz operators with a single Dirac delta distribution at the origin. Such fundamental solutions should have the properties that they are singular at the origin and bounded at the opposite pole of the hypersphere. Since homogeneous solutions of the Helmholtz equation are of the form
(\sin\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})^{-\mu}\big[c_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}^{(\pm,\pm)}{\sf P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{\pm\mu}(\pm\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})+c_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle 2$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}^{(\pm,\pm)}{\sf Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{\pm\mu}(\pm\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})\big]
, where
\mu=\frac{d}{2}-1
(see Section
3.3
), one should study
the behavior of these homogeneous solutions as
x\to-1^{+}
. In fact,
{\sf P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(-x)
is the unique solution to the associated Legendre differential equation with the required behavior as
x\to-1^{+}
(see Section
2.4.1
). In particular,
(\sin\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})^{-\mu}{\sf P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(-x)
has the required spherically symmetric regular behavior on the opposite pole.

Furthermore, as was seen in Section
4.1
, a fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
must also satisfy a total integral normalization property (
4.4
), due to applying the divergence theorem. An alternative integration constraint due to the divergence theorem on a hypersphere can be considered as follows. Consider the compact manifold with boundary by a ball of radius
R\epsilon
,
0<\epsilon\ll 1
, embedded within
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
with center at the origin
\theta=0
. Let
{\bf X}=\nabla{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})
and through (
4.2
), one has

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}{\bf X}=\Delta{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=-\delta_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\pm\beta^{2}{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}).

Now use the divergence theorem (
4.3
), and one obtains what we refer to as the
\epsilon
-ball integral constraint, namely

\displaystyle-1\pm\beta^{2}\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R\epsilon$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R\epsilon$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R\epsilon$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R\epsilon$}}}^{d}$}}}{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\mathrm{d}V=\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R\epsilon$}}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R\epsilon$}}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R\epsilon$}}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R\epsilon$}}}^{d-1}$}}}\left[\frac{1}{R}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\right]_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\theta=\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\theta=\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\theta=\epsilon$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\theta=\epsilon$}}}\mathrm{d}{\tilde{V}},

(4.17)

which can be verified directly. This is because for some differentiable function
u(\theta)
on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
,
\theta\in(0,\pi)
, the normal derivative in standard geodesic polar coordinates (
3.2
) is

\displaystyle\langle\nabla u(\theta),N\rangle=\frac{1}{R}\frac{\partial u(\theta)}{\partial\theta}.

Apart from these local behaviors, fundamental solutions of Helmholtz operators on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
may or may not have the desired limiting behaviors as
\beta\to 0
or in the flat-space limit
R\to\infty
. For instance, there does not exist a fundamental solution of Laplace’s equation on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
with non-vanishing total integral. Hence for fundamental solutions of a single Dirac delta distribution
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}
(
4.2
), the
\beta\to 0
limit should not exist. However, for an opposite antipodal fundamental solution
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}
(
4.5
), the
\beta\to 0
limit should exist, namely (
4.11
)

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta\to 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta\to 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta\to 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta\to 0$}}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}.

One would also expect that since all manifolds are locally Euclidean, that a fundamental solution of Helmholtz operators should look locally like a Euclidean fundamental solutions of Helmholtz operators (
4.8
), (
4.9
), namely

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R\to\infty}}{{\sim}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta$}}}^{d,\pm},\qquad{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R\to\infty}}{{\sim}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}.

Since the Sommerfeld radiation condition requirement does not apply to the Helmholtz operator
\big({-}\Delta+\beta^{2}\big)
, for this operator, problems associated with this requirement should not arise. In fact, we will see the above requirements for this operator is easily satisfied.

4.4.1
Fundamental solutions on
\boldsymbol{{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}}
for
\boldsymbol{\big({-}\Delta+\beta^{2}\big)}

In analogy to solutions of a damped pendulum, for the operator under study in this section, the following has been brought to our attention by one of the referees. If
\beta^{2}
is not constrained to be either positive or negative, physicists would call the cases: (1)
4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}<0
, “underdamped” or “oscillatory”; (2)
4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}>0
, “overdamped”; and (3)
4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}=0
, “critically damped”. A fundamental solution for
\big({-}\Delta+\beta^{2}\big)
will oscillate increasingly on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
when
\beta^{2}
is made increasingly negative, so that
4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}<0
is increasingly negative. However if
\beta^{2}
is made sufficiently positive, the oscillations in a fundamental solution of
\big({-}\Delta+\beta^{2}\big)
will disappear.

A fundamental solution with a single Dirac delta distribution at the origin for
\big({-}\Delta+\beta^{2}\big)
on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
is given in the following theorem.

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
d\in\{2,3,\ldots\}
. Define
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}\colon\big({\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\times{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\big)\setminus\big\{({\bf x},{\bf x})\colon{\bf x}\in{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\big\}\to{\mathbf{R}}
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}):=\frac{\Gamma(\nu+\mu+1)\Gamma(\mu-\nu)}{2^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}(\sin\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(-\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}),

(4.18)

where

\displaystyle\mu=\tfrac{d}{2}-1,\qquad\nu=\begin{cases}-{\tfrac{1}{2}}+{\tfrac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}},&\mathrm{if}\ (d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}\geq 0,\\
-{\tfrac{1}{2}}+\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}},&\mathrm{if}\ (d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}\leq 0,\end{cases}

(4.19)

\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}:=\cos^{-1}((\widehat{\bf x},{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}))
is the geodesic distance between
\widehat{\bf x}
and
{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}
on the hypersphere of unit radius
{\mathbf{S}}^{d}
,
\widehat{\bf x}={\bf x}/R
,
{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}={{\bf x}^{\prime}}/R
. Then
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}
is a fundamental solution
for the Helmholtz operator
\big({-}\Delta+\beta^{2}\big)
on
