{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
.

Proof.

Apart from a constant in
\nu
,
\mu
, we guess a form of a fundamental solution for this Helmholtz operator for a single Dirac delta distribution at the origin on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
. Hence our ansatz will be

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime}):=\frac{c(\nu,\mu){\sf P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(-x)}{\big(1-x^{2}\big)^{\frac{\mu}{2}}},

where
x=\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}
, and
c(\nu,\mu)
is constant in
x
. This constant, for instance, can be determined through the integral normalization requirement (
4.4
). We must verify

\displaystyle\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}}{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}.

(4.20)

Starting with the left-hand side of (
4.20
), and using (
2.51
), noting the standard volume integral

\displaystyle\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d-1}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d-1}$}}}\mathrm{d}{\rm vol}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}^{\prime}=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)}R^{d-1},

(4.21)

after simplifying, one requires that

\displaystyle c(\nu,\mu)=\frac{\Gamma(\nu+\mu+1)\Gamma(\mu-\nu)}{2^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}}.

All that remains is to demonstrate that (
4.18
) produces (
4.8
) in the flat-space limit,
R\to\infty
. In the flat-space limit
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}\sim\frac{\vartheta}{R}
and
\nu\sim-\tfrac{1}{2}+{\rm i}\beta R
, with
\vartheta\to\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|
. Using the uniform asymptotics given by (
2.64
), the demonstration is validated. This completes the proof of Theorem
4.8
.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

Note that (
2.47
) gives the strength of the cusp on the opposite pole (at
\theta=\pi
) for fundamental solutions on hyperspheres, such as that given in Theorem
4.8
.

Remark 4.10
.

If one considers the
\beta\sim 0
approximation for (
4.18
), we see that as
\beta\to 0
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\sim\mathcal{A}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})+\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+1}{2}\big)}{2\pi^{\frac{d+1}{2}}R^{d}\beta^{2}}.

In order to obtain this, we have used
[
27
, equation (14.5.18)]
, the binomial and small angle approximations for trigonometric functions. Hence
\lim\limits_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta\to 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta\to 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta\to 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta\to 0$}}}{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\infty
, and as one expects (see Section
4.1
) the
\beta\to 0
(Laplace) limit of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})
does not exist.

Remark 4.11
.

The
\epsilon
-ball integral constraint (
4.17
) for
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}
is satisfied. This can be verified by noting that as
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}\to 0^{+}
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\sim\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}-1\big)}{4\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}(R\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})^{d-2}},\qquad\frac{1}{R}\frac{\partial}{\partial\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}}{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\sim-\frac{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})}{2\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}(R\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})^{d-1}},

and using (
4.21
).

A fundamental solution with two opposite antipodal Dirac delta distributions, one at the origin and another on the opposite pole of
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, for
\big({-}\Delta+\beta^{2}\big)
is given in the following theorem.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
d\in\{2,3,\ldots\}
. Define
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}\colon\big({\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\times{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\big)\setminus\big\{({\bf x},{\bf x})\colon{\bf x}\in{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\big\}\to{\mathbf{R}}
by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}):=\frac{\Gamma(\nu+\mu+1)\Gamma(\mu-\nu)}{2^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}(\sin\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}{\mathsf{f}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}),

(4.22)

where
\mu
and
\nu
are given by (
4.19
),
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}=\cos^{-1}((\widehat{\bf x},{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}))
is the geodesic distance between
\widehat{\bf x}
and
{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}
on the hypersphere of unit radius
{\mathbf{S}}^{d}
,
\widehat{\bf x}={\bf x}/R
,

{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}={{\bf x}^{\prime}}/R
, and
{\mathsf{f}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{\mu}
is the odd Ferrers function defined by (
2.48
). Then
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}
is an opposite antipodal fundamental solution for the Helmholtz operator
\big({-}\Delta+\beta^{2}\big)
on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
.

Proof.

Since the opposite antipodal fundamental solution of the Helmholtz operator
\big({-}\Delta+\beta^{2}\big)
must be an odd function, it should be given through (
2.48
). Starting with (
4.22
) we then perform the flat-space limit
R\to\infty
by using the asymptotics given by (
2.70
), along with
\sin(\pi(\nu-\mu))\sim-\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm e}^{\pi\tau}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}\pi\mu}
; as in the proof of Theorem
4.8
, we must consider that
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}\sim\vartheta/R
,
\vartheta\sim\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|
,
{\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
. Note that the asymptotic contribution due to
-{\sf P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})
is negligible as compared to
{\sf P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(-\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})
. One can then easily see that (
4.22
) correctly approaches (
4.8
). Furthermore, since (
4.22
) is an odd function about
\theta=\tfrac{\pi}{2}

on the hypersphere
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, the integral normalization requirement (
4.4
) is clearly satisfied. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

If one considers the
\beta\sim 0
approximation for (
4.22
), we see that as
\beta\to 0
,

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\sim\mathcal{A}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\frac{(d-2)!}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}\sin^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}}{\sf Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}^{1-\frac{d}{2}}(\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}),

the opposite antipodal fundamental solution of Laplace’s equation on the hypersphere (
4.11
). In order to demonstrate this limit, we have used the binomial, small angle approximations for trigonometric functions, as well as (
2.42
).

Remark 4.14
.

The
\epsilon
-ball integral constraint (
4.17
) for
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}
is also satisfied. This can be verified in an identical fashion to Remark
4.11
while also noting that
{\sf P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(x)
and its derivative vanish as
x\to 1^{-}
,
\Re\mu>0
.

Remark 4.15
.

Note that the integral normalization requirement (
4.4
) is satisfied trivially since
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}
, is an odd function of
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}
about
\tfrac{\pi}{2}
.

4.4.2
Candidate fundamental solutions on
\boldsymbol{{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}}
for
\boldsymbol{\big({-}\Delta-\beta^{2}\big)}

Definition 4.16
.

Let
d\in\{2,3,\ldots\}
,
R,\beta>0
. Define the
candidate functions

\displaystyle{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}\colon\ \big({\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\times{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\big)\setminus\big\{({\bf x},{\bf x})\colon{\bf x}\in{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\big\}\to{\mathbf{R}},

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}\colon\ \big({\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\times{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\big)\setminus\big\{({\bf x},{\bf x})\colon{\bf x}\in{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}\big\}\to{\mathbf{C}},

as

\displaystyle{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}):=\frac{\Gamma(\nu+\mu+1)\Gamma(\mu-\nu)}{2^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}(\sin\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}\,\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(-\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}),

(4.23)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}):=\frac{\Gamma(\nu+\mu+1)}{\Gamma(\nu-\mu+1)R^{d-2}(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}(\sin\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}\left(\mathsf{Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})+{\rm i}\frac{\pi}{2}\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})\right)

\displaystyle\hphantom{{\mathfrak{S}}_{R,\beta}^{d,-}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}{}=\frac{\Gamma(\nu+\mu+1)\Gamma(\mu-\nu)}{2^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}(\sin\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}\left(\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(-\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})-{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\pi(\nu-\mu)}\mathsf{P}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})\right),

(4.24)

where

\displaystyle\mu=\tfrac{d}{2}-1,\qquad\nu=-{\tfrac{1}{2}}+{\tfrac{1}{2}}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}+(d-1)^{2}},

(4.25)

and
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}=\cos^{-1}([\widehat{\bf x},{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}])
is the geodesic distance between
\widehat{\bf x}
and
{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}
on the hypersphere of unit radius
{\mathbf{S}}^{d}
, with
\widehat{\bf x}={\bf x}/R
,
{\widehat{\bf x}^{\prime}}={{\bf x}^{\prime}}/R
.

Remark 4.17
.

Note that apart from a sign difference (due to our choice of a positive Laplace–Beltrami operator
-\Delta
) and the inclusion of the radius of curvature
R
of the hypersphere
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, the candidate function
{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})
is identical to that which was found by Liu & Ryan (2002)
[
20
, equation (5.1)]
and Szmytkowski (2007)
[
31
, equation (3.23)]
in terms of Gegenbauer functions (cf. (
2.37
)) for unit radius hyperspheres. Note that in order to convert the wavenumber given in
[
31
]
, so that it is the same as given here, one must take
\beta^{2}R^{2}=\lambda(\lambda+d-1)
, and therefore take

\displaystyle\lambda=-\frac{d-1}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}},

with the plus sign chosen to match the formulae given here.

Remark 4.18
.

The candidate function
{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
satisfies the integral normalization requirement (
4.4
), namely

\displaystyle\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}}{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}.

This follows straightforwardly using (
2.51
), (
4.23
), with (
4.25
).

Remark 4.19
.

The
\epsilon
-ball integral constraint (
4.17
) for
{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
is also satisfied. This can be verified by noting that as
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}\to 0^{+}
,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\sim\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}-1\big)}{4\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}(R\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})^{d-2}},\qquad\frac{1}{R}\frac{\partial}{\partial\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}}{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\sim-\frac{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2})}{2\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}(R\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})^{d-1}},

and using (
4.21
).

Remark 4.20
.

If one considers the small
\beta
approximation for
{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})
we see that as
\beta\to 0
,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\sim{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})-\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+1}{2}\big)}{2\pi^{\frac{d+1}{2}}R^{d}\beta^{2}}.

In order to obtain this we have used
[
27
, equation (14.5.18)]
, the binomial and small angle approximations for trigonometric functions. Hence
\lim\limits_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta\to 0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta\to 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta\to 0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta\to 0$}}}{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\infty
, and as one would expect (see Section
4.1
) the
\beta\to 0
(Laplace) limit of
{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})
does not exist.

Remark 4.21
.

The flat-space limit
R\to\infty
of the function
{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
should not exist. In fact, it oscillates wildly in this limit, as it approaches the following function

\displaystyle{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}\sim\frac{{\rm i}}{4}\left(\frac{\beta}{2\pi\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\left({\rm i}\cot\big(\pi\beta R+\tfrac{\pi(1-d)}{2}\big)J_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}(\beta\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|)+{\rm i}Y_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}(\beta\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|)\right),

where
{\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
.

On the other hand, the candidate function
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})
does approach the correct function in the flat-space
R\to\infty
limit (
4.9
), but does not obey the integral normalization
requirement (
4.4
).

Theorem 4.22
.

In the flat-space limit
R\to\infty
, the function
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
approaches the Euclidean fundamental solution of
\big({-}\Delta-\beta^{2}\big)
, namely (
4.9
)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\sim{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\frac{{\rm i}}{4}\left(\frac{\beta}{2\pi\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}^{(1)}(\beta\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|).

Proof.

In the flat-space limit
R\to\infty
, with geodesic distance
R\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}
bounded, and thus
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}\to 0
. Using (
2.52
), (
4.9
),
one has
\mu=\frac{d}{2}-1
,
\nu=-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}\sim\beta R
, and as
R\to\infty
,
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}\to 0^{+}
, that
\sin\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}\sim\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}
,
R\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}\sim\|\bf x-\bf x^{\prime}\|
, and

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})\sim{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}}),

since
(\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}/\sin\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})\sim 1
as
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}\to 0
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.23
.

The candidate function
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
does not satisfy the integral normalization requirement (
4.4
), namely

\displaystyle\int_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}$}}}{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\big(1-{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\pi(\nu-\mu)}\big).

This follows straightforwardly using (
4.24
), (
2.51
) with (
4.25
).

Remark 4.24
.

In the limit
\beta\to 0
, the candidate function
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
approaches a Laplace fundamental solution, namely (
4.9
), except that it differs from (
4.11
) by a purely imaginary constant (this satisfies the homogeneous Laplace equation), namely

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,0$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})={\mathcal{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})+\frac{{\rm i}\Gamma\big(\frac{d-1}{2}\big)}{4\pi^{\frac{d-1}{2}}R^{d-2}}.

Definition 4.25
.

In contrast to the situation for the operator
\big({-}\Delta+\beta^{2}\big)
on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
, we may also define the opposite antipodal candidate functions,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}):=\frac{\Gamma(\nu+\mu+1)\Gamma(\mu-\nu)}{2^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}(\sin\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}{\mathsf{f}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}),

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}):=\frac{\Gamma(\nu+\mu+1)\Gamma(\mu-\nu)\big(1+{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\pi(\nu-\mu)}\big)}{2^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}(\sin\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}{\mathsf{f}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\nu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nu$}}}^{-\mu}(\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}),

where
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
is constructed by converting (
4.24
) into an odd function using (
2.48
), and the variable definitions are as in Definition
4.16
.

Remark 4.26
.

The opposite antipodal candidate function
{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})
approaches the Laplace fundamental solution as
\beta\to 0^{+}
, namely (
4.11
)

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta\to 0^{+}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta\to 0^{+}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta\to 0^{+}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta\to 0^{+}$}}}{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})=\frac{(d-2)!}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}\sin^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}}{\sf Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}^{1-\frac{d}{2}}(\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}),

and in the flat-space limit it approaches

\displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}\sim\frac{{\rm i}}{4}\left(\frac{\beta}{2\pi\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\left(H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}}}^{(1)}(\beta\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|)-\big(1+{\rm i}\tan(\tfrac{\pi}{2}(\nu-\mu)\big)J_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}}}(\beta\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|)\right).

Remark 4.27
.

The opposite antipodal candidate function
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})
approaches the Laplace fundamental solution as
\beta\to 0^{+}
, namely (
4.11
)

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\beta\to 0^{+}$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\beta\to 0^{+}$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\beta\to 0^{+}$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta\to 0^{+}$}}}{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})=\frac{(d-2)!}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}\sin^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}}{\sf Q}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{d}{2}-1$}}}^{1-\frac{d}{2}}(\cos\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}),

and in the flat-space limit it approaches

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}\sim\frac{{\rm i}}{4}\left(\frac{\beta}{2\pi\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}-1}\left(H_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}}}^{(1)}(\beta\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|)-{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\pi(\nu-\mu)}J_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mu$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu$}}}(\beta\|{\bf x}-{{\bf x}^{\prime}}\|)\right).

Remark 4.28
.

Note that for both functions, the integral normalization requirement (
4.4
) is satisfied trivially since
{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
,
{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
are odd functions of
\rho_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle s$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle s$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s$}}}
about
\tfrac{\pi}{2}
.

Conjecture 4.29
.

Let
d\in\{2,3,\ldots\}
. Then the candidate function
{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})
is a fundamental solution for the Helmholtz operator
\big({-}\Delta-\beta^{2}\big)
on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
. Similarly, the candidate function
{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}({\bf x},{\bf x}^{\prime})
is an opposite antipodal fundamental solution for the Helmholtz operator
\big({-}\Delta-\beta^{2}\big)
on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
.

Conjecture
4.29
seems quite plausible since, unlike
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
,
{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
satisfies all the critical Remarks
4.17
–
4.20
. Apart from having the correct flat-space limit,
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
does not satisfy the integral normalization requirement (
4.4
) which is not allowed, as shown in Section
4.1
. The only critical property that

{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
satisfies is the correct Euclidean flat-space limit. The flat-space behavior of
{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
, displayed in Remark
4.21
, may well be due to imposing incorrect boundary conditions since
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
is a compact manifold without boundary, whereas Euclidean space is actually a noncompact manifold with boundary (at infinity). Furthermore,
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
is partially ‘antipodal’ to the source point (diverges at the source point as well as at the antipole), which is not a desirable property. It was also pointed out by one of the referees that due to the
\Gamma(\mu-\nu)
factor,
{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
will diverge when
\beta=0
, as is necessary (see Remark
4.19
), and at an infinite number of points in the underdamped (oscillatory) regime which correspond to the wavenumber being one of the eigenvalues of the Laplace–Beltrami operator on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
. Similarly,
{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
has all the right properties that an opposite antipodal fundamental solution of the Helmholtz operator
\big({-}\Delta-\beta^{2}\big)
on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
should satisfy, with the exception of the correct flat-space limit. Given a resolution of the flat-space limit for
{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
, the same problem will be solved for
{\mathsf{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,-}
. The ultimate resolution of this Conjecture
4.29
must also be associated with a correct and consistent formulation of the Sommerfeld radiation condition (
4.6
) on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}
.

5
Gegenbauer expansions in geodesic polar coordinates

For convenience, define the following degrees

\displaystyle\nu_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}:=\begin{cases}-{\tfrac{1}{2}}+{\tfrac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}},&\mathrm{if}\ (d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}\geq 0,\\
-{\tfrac{1}{2}}+\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sqrt{4\beta^{2}R^{2}-(d-1)^{2}}&\mathrm{if}\ (d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}\leq 0,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\nu_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}}:=-{\tfrac{1}{2}}+{\tfrac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(d-1)^{2}+4\beta^{2}R^{2}}.

In the boundary case,
(d-1)^{2}-4\beta^{2}R^{2}=0
, or equivalently,

\displaystyle\beta\mapsto\frac{d-1}{2R},\qquad\nu_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}\mapsto-\frac{1}{2},\qquad\nu_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle-$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle-$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle-$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}}\mapsto\frac{\sqrt{2}(d-1)-1}{2},

the wavenumber only has dependence on the dimension of the space
d
and the radius of curvature of the manifold
R
. In this case, the Ferrers and associated Legendre functions reduce to elementary or elementary transcendental functions. For instance, if
\mu=\frac{d}{2}-1\in{\mathbf{Z}}
(
d
even), then the fundamental solutions are given in terms of complete elliptic integrals (the Legendre functions are toroidal harmonics, and the equivalent for the Ferrers functions). Alternatively, if
\mu
is a half odd integer (
d
odd), then the Ferrers functions and associated Legendre functions reduce to trigonometric and hyperbolic functions respectively. Given starting points for various degrees and orders, a lattice of degrees and orders which satisfy these properties can be obtained using recurrence relations for these functions, namely

[
27
, equations (14.10.3), (14.10.6) and (14.10.7)]
,
[
27
, equations (14.10.1) and (14.10.2)]
.

In fact, recent results due to Maier
[
23
, Theorem 6.1]
have shown that the space of Ferrers and associated Legendre functions is much more rich than was previously expected. In fact if the orders are integers and the degrees differ by
\pm 1/r

(r=2,3,4,6)
from an integer, then the resulting Legendre functions are also given in terms of complete elliptic integrals of the first and second kind!

Define the constants

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}:=\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)}{2(d-2)\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}},\qquad\mathsf{b}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,\pm}:=\frac{2^{\mu}\Gamma(\mu)\Gamma(\nu_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}}+\mu+1)\Gamma(\mu-\nu_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\pm$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\pm$}}})}{2^{\frac{d}{2}+2}\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}R^{d-2}}.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
d\geq 3
,
R>0
,
\beta\in{\mathbf{R}}
,
r,r^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)

r\neq r^{\prime}

. Then

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\frac{{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}\pi(\frac{d}{2}-1)}{\sf a}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R$}}}^{d}}{(\sinh r\sinh r^{\prime})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}

(5.3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}({\bf x},{{\bf x}^{\prime}})=\frac{{\sf b}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle R,\beta$}}}^{d,+}}{(\sin\theta\sin\theta^{\prime})^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}\sum_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle l=0$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle l=0$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle l=0$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l=0$}}}^{\infty}(2l+d-2)\left(\nu_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}+\tfrac{d}{2}\right)_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle l$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}\big(\tfrac{d}{2}-1-\nu_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle+$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle+$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle+$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}\big)_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle l$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}

Proof.

We have already shown in the flat-space limit that

5.1
Azimuthal Fourier series in two dimensions

It is interesting to consider how we can obtain azimuthal Fourier expansions for our fundamental solutions. For the particular case where a fundamental solution is given in terms of an associated Legendre function of the second kind, we can use the addition theorem (
2.33
) for the two-dimensional case. One has the following azimuthal Fourier expansions in
d=2
.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let
\beta\in{\mathbf{R}}
,
r,r^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)
,
r\neq r^{\prime}
,
\theta,\theta^{\prime},\phi-\phi^{\prime}\in[0,\pi]
. Then

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\displaystyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\textstyle R,\beta$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle l$}}{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle
